Title: Of the Markes of the Children of God, and of their Comforts in Afflictions. To the faithfull of the Low Countrie
Creator: Jean Taffin (title page: "John Taffin"); "ouerseene againe and augmented by the Author, and translated out of French by Anne Prowse" (title page)
Publisher: London: Imprinted by Felix Kingston for Thomas Man, dwelling in Pater noster Row, at the signe of the Talbot
Publication Date: 1599
Rights: Public Domain (published 1599; public domain by date under the project's 95-year rule)
Source: Internet Archive item bim_early-english-books-1475-1640_of-the-markes-of-the-chi_taffin-jean_1599, file bim_early-english-books-1475-1640_of-the-markes-of-the-chi_taffin-jean_1599_djvu.txt, fetched directly (curl, raw bytes, the item's own _djvu.txt full-text derivative) by the library/source-research thread (Era VII session, world agent VI.9), 2026-10-16; host rights statement: the item carries no possible-copyright-status field (Internet Archive early-English-books microfilm collection, contributor Internet Archive); the 1599 imprint is read from the title page in the OCR itself
Provenance note: Header prepended; the OCR text follows unchanged apart from long s (U+017F) replaced by s, 6100 occurrences. Rights basis restated: a book printed in London in 1599 is public domain by date.
Content note: The whole volume: title page, Anne Prowse's dedication to the Countess of Warwick, and Taffin's treatise (first published in French, 1586, for the Reformed of the Low Countries; Taffin was a Walloon pastor of the Dutch Reformed church). Microfilm OCR of a 1599 roman-type print: sampled at 10/30/50/70/90 % and found readable with scattered character errors and marginal scripture references run into the text; usable to quote once each quote is checked against the page image. A 1609 printing of the same translation exists as Internet Archive item bim_early-english-books-1475-1640_of-that-markes-of-the-ch_taffin-j_1609, not vendored.

the righteous shall suftaine: 
By Gods determination, 
whilst here they doe remaine. 
Which grieuous is and irkesome both, 
for flesh and blood to beare : 
Because by nature we are loath, 
to want our pleasure heere. 
And eke because our enemie, 
thar auncienrt deadly foe, 
Sathan,with cruell tyrannie, 
the worker of our woe : 
Doth still prouoke the wicked sort, 
in finne which doc delight : | 
To please themselues and make grear sport, 
ro vexe vs with despite. 
Yertdoe the righteous by the crosle, 
moe blessed things obtaine: 
Than any way can bethe losle, 
the dolor,or the paine, 
The losle is that which in few dayes 
would pasle, fade and decay 
Euen of it selfe : the gaine alwayes | 
can no man take away.
